our best faculties will be gratified? wealth, fame, health, and all
other good things will flow in a stream, and our delight remain
permanent?" asked Doctor Lewis at this stage of the argument.

<span about="#mwt19"> </span>

<span id="c17_Contending_Forces_Chapter_17.xhtml#id-294"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="294"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/302"
page-index="302" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/302"></span>"Not
necessarily obedience to God," replied the free-thinker. "Certain causes
bring certain effects, without reference to a presiding deity."

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"That’s it!" said the Southerner, *sotto voce,* to his next neighbor.
"Negroes are all alike with regard to religion; ignorant, thieving,
dirty and lazy, but withal crammed full of religious enthusiasm. You
should know them as I do—shouting, screaming, frothing at the mouth with
the outpourings of the ‘spirit,' and as soon as they are outside of the
church door, robbing hen-roosts and watermelon patches. Bah!"

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

Low as his words were spoken, they caught the ear of Will. His flashing
black eyes were turned upon the speaker as he said with a courtly bow:

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"Granted that all you say is true, still the Negro does not sin against
ages of accumulated knowledge of right and wrong. It is not absolute
incapacity in the Negro either, but mere want of information, arising
from lack of intelligent exercise of the perceptive faculties. These
faults you speak of are but the remnants of an old irresponsible life.
The majority of our race has turned aside forever from the old beaten
paths of slavery into the undiscovered realms of free thought and free
action. Some of the race may abuse the newly acquired <span
id="c17_Contending_Forces_Chapter_17.xhtml#id-295"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="295"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/303"
page-index="303" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/303"></span>liberty
by petty pilfering or an overflow of religious enthusiasm, but the Negro
has *changed,* whether you realize it or not; *times* have changed, and
the Negro with them."

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

There was a murmur of applause as he finished speaking.

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"Well," returned the Southerner, "we will grant that some of you are
making progress, but you cannot claim that as a race you are capable of
becoming such examples of manhood as you and your friends. To most of
you the mystery of government will always remain a mystery; and the hope
of assimilating many things which are second nature to the white man,
will never become a reality to your race."

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"I believe that the same rules which govern all races will be applicable
to mine," returned Will. "If men are rude and foolish, down they must
go. When at last in any race a new principle appears, an idea, *that*
conserves it. Ideas only save races. If the black man is feeble and
impotent, unimportant to the existing races—not on a parity with the
best races, the black man must serve and be exterminated. *But,* if he
carries within his bosom the element of a new and coming civilization,
he will survive and play his part."

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"Hear, hear!" cried the foreign secretary of legation, applauding
vigorously, delighted at <span
id="c17_Contending_Forces_Chapter_17.xhtml#id-296"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="296"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/304"
page-index="304" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/304"></span>the
strong, combative style in which the brave young fellow met the ancient
enemy of his race. "No race is hopelessly lost to the world of progress
that produces such manly specimens within fifty years of emancipation."

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"England is troubled over the fact that the two races do not mix, and to
all appearances never will," now joined in Mr. Withington. "There seems
to be no common ground between them; they cannot live together
harmoniously. Can you, gentlemen, explain this to me? I should like to
carry back with me, when I return, a clear realization of the facts in
the case as viewed by each party to the question. That is part of my
mission in this country."

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"It is true," said Doctor Lewis, "that our condition is alarming to a
degree. Over ten thousand graves made since the war by mob violence, are
dotting the South, and of these ninety per cent are the graves of black
men."

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"You do not get these facts abroad because newspaper reports are
doctored by local Southern writers who participate in the lynchings.
Free speech and public discussions are not allowed. In the South you
must think and speak as the mob dictates," here chimed in John Langley.

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"But surely you have constitutional equity <span
id="c17_Contending_Forces_Chapter_17.xhtml#id-297"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="297"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/305"
page-index="305" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/305"></span>before
the law? you can gain redress from the Federal government?"

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"Constitutional equity is a political fiction," laughed Will.

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"Reconstruction is a failure, then?" asked Mr. Withington.

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"Reconstruction is not a failure, although the whole South will tell you
so, and the North is being persuaded to so believe; the Negro was the
living force which rent asunder the old methods of the slave-holding
states and brought them out into the healthful light of decent,
God-fearing living. But they are trying to take all the credit for the
good we may have done them, from us. The lines are drawn more sharply
than before the emancipation. Constitutional amendments are dead
letters; the ballot-box is nil," replied Will.

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"All of which goes to show that race is stronger than law," broke in the
Southerner.

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"Rather that barbarism is superior to civilization."

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"What about the crime of rape?" asked Mr. Withington.

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"In nine cases out of ten," replied Lewis, "you will find that the Negro
is guiltless of this awful crime. It is brought forward to alienate the
sympathy of all decent men from us. It is a crime that strikes the home
ties, and as such <span
id="c17_Contending_Forces_Chapter_17.xhtml#id-298"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="298"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/306"
page-index="306" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/306"></span>is the
most deadly weapon that has yet been used against us. We invite
investigation in this direction, and you will then find that it is *not*
a characteristic of the black man, although *it is* of the white man of
the South."

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"You cannot prove your assertions!" exclaimed the Southerner, white with
passion.

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"I can."

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"Name a case."

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"John Thomas of Georgia."

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"He was guilty, and deserved his fate. You, gentlemen, must have heard
the story of the atrocities committed by that man. The woman’s husband
was brutally murdered and she was assaulted. We made the fate of the
black devil a terrible example for the rest of his kind in Georgia."

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"Do you not. know that that story has been proven false all through? It
was this way, gentlemen: After the man Thomas was lynched, the colored
people were convinced of his innocence. A sum of money was raised by
subscription to hire the services of a reputable detective to
investigate the matter. He visited the scene of the alleged crime, and
it was found that Thomas worked for Cragen on his farm. Thomas heard
that his mother was sick, and wished to visit her; having no money, he
applied to Cragen for his pay. This enraged <span
id="c17_Contending_Forces_Chapter_17.xhtml#id-299"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="299"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/307"
page-index="307" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/307"></span>the
latter, and he drew his revolver to shoot the Negro. Quick as a flash
Thomas seized an axe which happened to be lying near, and struck Cragen,
killing him instantly. He then fled to the woods. Mrs. Cragen says that
Thomas did not assault her; she never saw him."

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"And for defending himself they lynched him?" said Mr. Withington, with
a look on his face which expressed incredulous astonishment, combined
with disgust.

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"Yes; that was the detective’s report, given under oath. What the man
suffered at the hands of the lynchers could only be equalled by the
sufferings of the martyrs of Nero’s time. If every case of Negro
lynching could be investigated, we should discover fearful discrepancies
between the story of the mob and the real truth."

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"That does not prove that white men are ravishers," said the Southerner,
returning to the charge.

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"Oh," replied Will, with a significant shrug of the shoulders, "take the
representatives of my race who are with you tonight. How did we get our
complexions, soft curls and regular features? Our ancestors were black,
flat-featured, and had many other racial marks. Your race does not
intermarry with Negroes, does <span
id="c17_Contending_Forces_Chapter_17.xhtml#id-300"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="300"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/308"
page-index="308" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/308"></span>it?
That being the case, the answer is self-evident."

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"Do you not think, then, that under the circumstances the Government
ought to assist the Negro to leave the country?" asked Mr. Withington.

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"Never!" cried Will, with glowing countenance, "Never! Here where we
have been so outrageously maligned, let us refute the charge like men!"

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"That sounds well," said the Southerner with a sneer, "but how will you
do it?"

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"By using the methods of the South," replied Will quickly; "create
sentiment *for* the race and *against* its detractors."

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"And if you get nothing else out of it, the agitators will do a good
business and fill their pockets with money."

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

"Agitation will do much," said Will calmly, unruffled by the other’s
display of anger. "It gave us freedom; it will give us manhood. The
peace, dignity and honor of this nation rises or falls with the Negro.
Frederic Douglas once said: ‘Ultimately this nation will be composite.
There is a strong demand—a growing demand—for a government capable of
protecting all its citizens—rich and poor, white and black—alike.’ The
causes for such a government still exist. It remains to be seen if the
prophecy will be fulfilled."

<span about="#mwt25"> </span>

<span id="c17_Contending_Forces_Chapter_17.xhtml#id-301"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="301"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/309"
page-index="309" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/309"></span>One of
the founders of the American Academy at Rome was present, and
conversation now turned upon the development of American talent in art.
The political aspect of Great Britain in South Africa and the
complications likely to arise therefrom was another interesting topic,
but under it all the sentiment expressed and the words spoken by the
representatives of an humble race that night lingered in the heart of
each scholar and thinker who had heard them.

<span about="#mwt39"> </span>

Will had a pressing engagement, and was obliged to excuse himself to the
president of the club. He withdrew as quietly as possible. Those nearest
him shook him warmly by the hand, and told him that they hoped to hear
from him again, and soon, on the same interesting topic.

<span about="#mwt39"> </span>

Mr. Withington now attached himself to John Langley for the remainder of
the evening, seeming well pleased to find himself in such company. As
they bade each other good-night at the close of the festivities he said
to John, as he placed his card in his hand:

<span about="#mwt39"> </span>

"I leave tomorrow for Canada, on business connected with the Home
Office. I may never return to this country, but I want you to believe
that I am deeply interested in the welfare of your people. Write to me
whatever comes <span id="c17_Contending_Forces_Chapter_17.xhtml#id-302"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="302"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/310"
page-index="310" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/310"></span>up of
importance to the race, and I will do what I can to make sentiment in my
own country for you, and as far as money will go, you shall have the
benefit of the help it will give."

<span about="#mwt39"> </span>

John bade him good-by, and even his conservative heart was warmed by
contact with so good a man. After he had reached his room he took the
card from his pocket, and before putting it in a place of safety read
the address:

<span about="#mwt39"> </span>

<span class="mw-empty-elt"></span>

<span style="font-size:120%;">CHARLES MONTFORT-WITHINGTON, M. P.</span>

<span class="smallcaps">Blankshire,</span>  
<span class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:4em"></span><span
class="smallcaps">England.</span>

<span about="#mwt39"> </span>

For a long time he sat there staring at the card in his hand. Where had
he heard that name before? "Charles Montfort!" Why, it was the family
name of Mrs. Smith! Instantly the words of Madam Frances came to his
mind, and a great light seemed to illumine his thoughts: "When you are
at that table in the company of those men, you shall have the answer to
one of the questions you would ask me."

<span about="#mwt39"> </span>

How often he had listened to the story told by Ma Smith with an
incredulous smile. It had seemed to him the idle boasting of a mind
entering its dotage. Charles Montfort!—and from England! Heavens! if
there should be truth in this fairy tale!

<span about="#mwt39"> </span>

<span id="c18_Contending_Forces_Chapter_18.xhtml"></span>

<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt1"></span>

<span id="c18_Contending_Forces_Chapter_18.xhtml#id-303"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="303"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/311"
page-index="311" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/311" about="#mwt4"
typeof="mw:Transclusion"></span>

<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt5"
typeof="mw:Transclusion"></span>

CHAPTER XVIII.

<span style="font-size:92%;">WHAT EASTER SUNDAY BROUGHT.</span>

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

  

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

<span class="mw-empty-elt"></span>

<span class="ws-poem-line"><span class="wst-tooltip wst-tooltip-dash"
title="Quote is from 'The Spanish Student' Act 1, Scene 2, by Henry Wadsworth Longfellow">'<span
class="smallcaps">Twas</span> Easter Sunday. The full-blossomed
trees</span><span class="ws-poem-break">  
</span></span><span class="ws-poem-line">Filled all the air with
fragrance and with joy.<span class="ws-poem-break">  
</span></span><span class="ws-poem-line ws-poem-right">—[<span
class="smallcaps">Longfellow</span>](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Author:Henry_Wadsworth_Longfellow "Author:Henry Wadsworth Longfellow")<span
class="ws-poem-break">  
</span></span><span class="ws-poem-break">  
</span>

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

<span class="smallcaps">The</span> days passed very quietly at No. 500 D
Street. The excitement occasioned by the fair, and the extraordinary
agitation which had attended the meeting of the American Colored League,
had somewhat abated. Softer thoughts and feelings had taken possession
of the public mind, for it was very near to the celebration of Easter.
The two girls, Dora and Sappho, felt restrained in their intercourse
because, although by tacit consent nothing was said of John's
treacherous conduct toward Dora, each knew that the other had discovered
it. Dora felt aggrieved and ill-treated; and who could blame her? Doctor
Lewis was drawn into the trouble through his attachment to Dora. He
pitied her injured feelings; he knew that if she were free and he
offered her his love, it would be accepted, and he was not too proud to
have done this if it had been an honorable act. He knew that it would
not be an honorable act to <span
id="c18_Contending_Forces_Chapter_18.xhtml#id-304"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="304"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/312"
page-index="312" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/312"></span>ask
her to break her promise to Langley at this stage of the drama, but
within his heart hope grew stronger each day. He saw that John was
hopelessly infatuated with Sappho; he saw Dora knew this and resented
the wrong done herself, although as yet she could hardly put her
thoughts into words. Day-dreams began to break in upon the dull monotony
of his methodical existence; he watched the signs of growing discontent
with all a lover's impatience to grasp the happiness which he felt
would, after a time, be his. He was willing to work, to wait, to serve,
if only he might claim her love at last. Oh, the nobleness of a good
man’s love! If women would but prize it, how many more happy homes we
should see, and how much brighter the world and life would be. Toward
Langley he was savage; and then, too, he was puzzled.

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

One night they were all invited to attend a reception given at the house
of a friend. He was with Dora and John. Dora addressed a remark to John,
and receiving no answer, turned to look in his face for the reason. His
gaze was fixed upon Sappho, who was surrounded by every eligible man in
the house. And such a look! love—hatred—tenderness—the gamut of passion
was disclosed by the look bent upon the unconscious girl before <span
id="c18_Contending_Forces_Chapter_18.xhtml#id-305"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="305"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/313"
page-index="313" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/313"></span>them.
Dora shuddered as if a chill had struck her, and turning to Lewis held a
cold and trembling little hand appealingly toward him. He took it gladly
into his firm and loving clasp, and tenderly asked her if she would like
to go home. She grasped the offer instantly with evident relief. She
left John’s side to seek the ladies’ dressing-room for her wraps; he,
absorbed in thoughts of Sappho, did not miss her presence. Lewis sent
word to Will that he was taking Dora home because she was feeling very
tired. He apologized to the hostess, engineered all the delicate details
of social life with neatness and dispatch, and met the harassed girl at
the foot of the staircase, ready to lead her home without arousing
disagreeable comment.

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

"What does the beggar mean?" he asked himself more than once that night.
"He does not seem inclined to give Dora up, and yet he is completely
‘gone’ over the other one." But, like a prudent man, he said nothing; he
kept his own counsel and watched.

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

Since the meeting at which Sappho had fainted, the girl had grown more
silent and depressed. People said: "Yes, she is beautiful, but so cold
and silent."

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

She and Will had scarcely spoken since the night of the fair, but each
felt and knew that <span
id="c18_Contending_Forces_Chapter_18.xhtml#id-306"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="306"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/314"
page-index="314" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/314"></span>it
would be but a short time before the story of their love must be settled
for good and all. So the days crept by and brought the crisis of these
lives, so strangely interwoven.

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

<span class="wst-asterisks tiInherit">•<span class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•</span>

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

Easter Sunday fell early in April that year. The city had donned its
gayest attire to welcome the return of the celebration of Christ’s
triumphal resurrection. The morning service was ended. The exultant
organ peals, the glorious strains of hymns of victory poured forth by
highly trained voices in hundreds of augmented church choirs all over
the city, still seemed to vibrate upon the listening air even after the
churches had given up their crowds of worshippers. Sappho had been to
the mission church of St. Augustine, on P. Street, at the West End. Here
the good Fathers of the Mission of St. John had reared a church, and
elected to bend their efforts particularly to the salvation and
upraising of the poorest of God’s children. This church, with its
beautiful silver chimes, given by a wealthy Boston woman in
commemoration of the life and services of the lamented Bishop Brooks, is
destined to stand for ages as a memorial of the deeds of philanthropy
which have always been a distinguishing feature of the true spirit of
Massachusetts.

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

The sound of the bells lingered with her like <span
id="c18_Contending_Forces_Chapter_18.xhtml#id-307"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="307"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/315"
page-index="315" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/315"></span>a
benediction. For the first time in weeks a tranquil mind was hers. The
spirit of the day was upon her in full measure; to her the risen Christ
was a reality, and his triumph over sin and death was to her bruised
spirit a promise and a blessing. Stepping lightly along she gained the
Public Garden and crossed the rustic bridge, skirting the lake as she
walked toward the Arlington-street entrance. Many admiring glances
followed the graceful figure, clad in its fresh, jaunty, tailor-made
suit of gray. The strong wind kissed the pure face and gently lifted the
clustering curls from the white brow, where Cupid lurked in every curve
and delicate thread of gold. The Garden was very beautiful this year; it
had blossomed with more than its usual luxuriance for the annual
festival. Beds of crocuses, gaudy tulips and hyacinths dotted the
landscape side by side with the modest violet and exquisite pansy. God’s
smile was everywhere. April’s sun and showers had blessed the earth, and

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

<span typeof="mw:Entity"> </span>

<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt5"></span>

<span class="mw-empty-elt"></span>

<span class="ws-poem-line"><span class="wst-fqm"
style="float:left; text-align:right; margin-left:calc(0em - 3em); width:3em; ">"</span>Cold
Nature, by his amorous kiss <span class="ws-poem-break">  
</span></span><span class="ws-poem-line">Stung sweetly, stirred his
limbs and felt <span class="ws-poem-break">  
</span></span><span class="ws-poem-line">A thrill of immemorial
bliss."<span class="ws-poem-break">  
</span></span><span class="ws-poem-break">  
</span>

<span typeof="mw:Entity"> </span>

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

Someone sat upon a garden seat awaiting her coming. Far down the mall he
espied the solitary figure. ’Twas she herself! As he gazed <span
id="c18_Contending_Forces_Chapter_18.xhtml#id-308"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="308"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/316"
page-index="316" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/316"></span>upon
the advancing figure of the girl he loved he felt his heart swell within
him, and tender thrills of joy filled his soul, too full for utterance.
He noticed that she wore a bunch of violets today, instead of her
favorite Jacks. They were more in keeping with the sentiment of the day.
There they rested in a splendid cluster upon her breast—modest and
beautifully fragrant. "As sweet as a violet herself," he thought, as he
murmured to himself:

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

<span typeof="mw:Entity"> </span>

<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt5"></span>

<span class="mw-empty-elt"></span>

<span class="ws-poem-line"><span class="wst-fqm"
style="float:left; text-align:right; margin-left:calc(0em - 3em); width:3em; ">"</span>The
breeze runs riot with thy charms, <span class="ws-poem-break">  
</span></span><span class="ws-poem-line">O faint, delicious, springtime
violet!"<span class="ws-poem-break">  
</span></span><span class="ws-poem-break">  
</span>

<span typeof="mw:Entity"> </span>

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

As a man Will Smith was not ashamed of the passion which consumed him.
An honorable love has its own subtle charm. Now was his opportunity! The
almost deserted paths were secure from intrusion. She was not
indifferent to him; today should end suspense. He would have her answer.

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

Sappho caught sight of him directly she entered the path. She
hesitated—turned—then stood still, as if conscious of his resolution. He
saw her hesitation, and read her thoughts by virtue of that harmony
which is love’s perfection. He arose and stood before her, hat in hand.
His apparent coolness and self-possession did what he intended it
should: restored her calmness; and when she placed <span
id="c18_Contending_Forces_Chapter_18.xhtml#id-309"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="309"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/317"
page-index="317" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/317"></span>her
hand in his and responded to his cheery greeting, she was herself again.

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

"Do not hurry away," he said, as she turned as if to resume her walk.
"Let us sit a moment and enjoy this charming view of beautiful Boston."

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

She stood a moment gazing at the fair prospect spread out before them.

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

"How peaceful, beautiful and calm the landscape seems. If earth be so
fair, what must be the beauty of heaven?" she said, after a slight
pause.

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

"Indeed, yes," replied Will, as he glanced at the fair face beside him
with something like awe. For a moment he felt that he stood in the
presence of a saint; earthly passion seemed out of place.

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

After a moment's silence she seated herself, mechanically protesting
that she had promised Dora to help her with her Sunday-school class at
half-past one.

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

"Never mind," said Will, "it is real Sunday-school work for you to look
after me a bit. I’m sure I need Bible lessons." At this they both
laughed, and then Sappho asked:

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

"Where ought the lesson to be?"

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

She saw instantly by the change in his expression that she had given him
his opening, and immediately lost the self-possession she had just
gained.

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

<span id="c18_Contending_Forces_Chapter_18.xhtml#id-310"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="310"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/318"
page-index="318" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/318"></span><span
style="padding-right:.2em;">"</span>'And Jacob loved Rachel; and said, I
will serve thee seven years for thy younger daughter,<span
style="padding-right:.2em;">'</span>" quoted Will softly, as he looked
at the gentle face and downcast eyes beside him. Sappho said nothing,
but gazed intently at the landscape with unseeing eyes, as she nervously
clasped and unclasped her delicately gloved hands. Suddenly she felt one
small hand drawn within a strong, warm clasp.

<span about="#mwt28"> </span>

"There are shadows on your face; why are they there? You think all kinds
of sad thoughts. Tell them to me, Sappho."

<span about="#mwt28"> </span>

"Nay," she said at length, "why should I? they are not clear even to
myself. You would not understand my vagaries. Why should you be bothered
with them?"

<span about="#mwt28"> </span>

"Because, Sappho, my beautiful one, my darling, I love you—surely you
know it—I have loved you from the first moment that I saw you. Be my
wife, my blessing."

<span about="#mwt28"> </span>

She did not answer; her face had grown very white.

<span about="#mwt28"> </span>

"Have I frightened you? Do not think me cruel, Sappho; you do not know
how much I love you."

<span about="#mwt28"> </span>

She was still silent, but another look satisfied him that she was not
angry. The gloved hand stopped fluttering, and lay quietly within his
clasp.

<span about="#mwt28"> </span>

<span id="c18_Contending_Forces_Chapter_18.xhtml#id-311"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="311"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/319"
page-index="319" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/319"></span>"You
do not think me presumptuous? You are not angry? Tell me that you love
me."

<span about="#mwt30"> </span>

"Wait one moment," she said; and then he saw that she was trembling
violently.

<span about="#mwt30"> </span>

"Don’t you care for me a little?" he asked anxiously, "or have I been
living in a fool’s paradise the past two months?"

<span about="#mwt30"> </span>

"I do care for you," she whispered, in a voice so low that only a lover
could have heard it.

<span about="#mwt30"> </span>

"Ah!" he cried impetuously; "then I will never let you go!"

<span about="#mwt30"> </span>

"Ah, Will, I wonder if it is right for me to love you, or to allow you
to love me?"

<span about="#mwt30"> </span>

"Most decidedly it is right. Why do you doubt?"

<span about="#mwt30"> </span>

"You have known me such a short time," she protested.

<span about="#mwt30"> </span>

"I know that you are the dearest girl in all the world to me," he
replied, holding her hand still more tightly clasped. "You are mine now,
and nothing shall part us. I shall always remember this day as the
happiest of my life. You do not know how dearly I love you."

<span about="#mwt30"> </span>

"I shall know if you persist in telling me so often," she said, smiling
at his impetuosity.

<span about="#mwt30"> </span>

"Heaven bless you, Sappho, my dearest. I shall never tire of ringing the
changes on love."

<span about="#mwt30"> </span>

"Why should you care so much for me?" she asked wistfully. "I have no
family—no friends—no money"—

<span about="#mwt30"> </span>

<span id="c18_Contending_Forces_Chapter_18.xhtml#id-312"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="312"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/320"
page-index="320" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/320"></span>"I
have family enough for both of us," he returned quickly. "My mother
loves you, my sister adores you; what more is there to be desired?"

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

"But there are things which you ought to know—things connected with the
past"—

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

"I do not care for the past," he broke in; "all I ask is that you love
me above all other men, as I adore you above all other women."

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

She was carried away by the vehemence of his wooing. All obstacles
seemed, indeed, but trifles before this all-absorbing, passionate, eager
warmth of youth’s first love. Poor soul! poor starved and storm-beaten
heart! Something of life that was dead leaped again into existence and
loosened the icy hand that had for years locked up the fountain of
youthful joy. She felt drawn out of herself as she looked into the eyes
of love and met the luminous light which transfigured the face of her
lover.

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

<span typeof="mw:Entity"> </span>

<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt32"></span>

<span class="mw-empty-elt"></span>

<span class="ws-poem-line"><span class="wst-fqm"
style="float:left; text-align:right; margin-left:calc(0em - 3em); width:3em; ">"</span>Ask
me no more; thy fate and mine are sealed:<span class="ws-poem-break">  
</span></span><span class="ws-poem-line">I strove against the stream and
all in vain;<span class="ws-poem-break">  
</span></span><span class="ws-poem-line">Let the great river take me in
the main:<span class="ws-poem-break">  
</span></span><span class="ws-poem-line">No more, dear love, for at a
touch I yield;<span class="ws-poem-break">  
</span></span><span class="ws-poem-line"><span class="ws-poem-indent"
style="width:3em"><span typeof="mw:Entity"> </span><span
typeof="mw:Entity"> </span><span typeof="mw:Entity"> </span></span>Ask
me no more.”<span class="ws-poem-break">  
</span></span><span class="ws-poem-break">  
</span>

<span typeof="mw:Entity"> </span>

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

Silently she turned to him and placed the other hand within his clasp,
and burst into a tempest of weeping.

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

<span id="c18_Contending_Forces_Chapter_18.xhtml#id-313"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="313"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/321"
page-index="321" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/321"></span>"Sappho!"
he cried, in a transport of delight, as he tried to dry her tears; "my
darling, my wife!"

<span about="#mwt36"> </span>

O happy Easter Day!

<span about="#mwt36"> </span>

They sat there a long time in heart communion, reviewing the history of
their short acquaintance, and the wonderful thing that had happened to
them. They planned that Sappho should stay with Will’s mother while he
went abroad, and then when his course was finished they would be married
immediately upon his return. They would keep their sweet secret a little
while. They could not share this hallowed day with others. It was
consecrated and holy to them because of its dear association with the
hidden stream of their united lives.

<span about="#mwt36"> </span>

<span class="wst-asterisks tiInherit">•<span class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•</span>

<span about="#mwt36"> </span>

John Langley was returning from an errand down town, and attracted by
the beauty of the morning, determined to cross the Garden on his way to
the South End. Suddenly, beneath the trees he espied the lovers. He
paused a moment. They were so much absorbed in each other that they had
not seen him. He turned aside into a path which ran very near to them on
one side, and paused beneath the shadow of some friendly trees where he
was secure from observation and could watch their movements unseen.

<span about="#mwt36"> </span>

<span id="c18_Contending_Forces_Chapter_18.xhtml#id-314"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="314"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/322"
page-index="322" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/322"></span>The
happy light on Will’s expressive countenance did not escape him; he
noted, too, the shy happiness and deep, tender love which shone in
Sappho’s eyes. His heart was on fire with hot, jealous rage, and he
resolved that their marriage should never take place. He was not sure of
Sappho’s past, but he would try a bold manœuvre and trust to luck. He
would accuse her as though certain of the facts. If that did not work,
he would try some other game. He who had laughed at love without money,
found himself even contemplating the sacrifice of one for the other, if
dishonest means should not succeed in winning her. He watched them for a
time, and then, judging by their movements that they were about to leave
the spot, turned reluctantly away,, planning ways and means of bringing
his schemes to a successful issue.

<span about="#mwt39"> </span>

<span class="wst-asterisks tiInherit">•<span class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•</span>

<span about="#mwt39"> </span>

Sappho had been taking her meals with the family lately, but tonight she
excused herself from dinner on the plea of a headache. Will had gone
back to the hotel.

<span about="#mwt39"> </span>

"Headache must be a great cosmetic," said Dora slyly. She had seen that
Will and Sappho returned from morning church together and from certain
signs had drawn her own conclusions about how matters stood between
<span id="c18_Contending_Forces_Chapter_18.xhtml#id-315"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="315"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/323"
page-index="323" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/323"></span>them.
"I never remember seeing you look better or more beautiful than you do
tonight." Sappho laughed joyously as she ran lightly up the stairs to
her room. Tomorrow they would know her secret.

<span about="#mwt39"> </span>

She glanced at her mirror as she passed it. Where was the usually pale,
sad face that it reflected? They called her cold and proud. What would
the world say if it could see that brilliant, vivid, flashing beauty
that the mirror gave back to her astonished gaze? Truly love was a
wonderful cosmetic. She sank into the inviting willow rocker, and for a
while gave herself up to the happiness which was so intense as to be
almost painful. There was no thought of evil mingled with the joy that
wrapped her round about. No forebodings assailed her; the intoxication
of the moment was complete. She was lost in dreamland as she sat there
thinking of her lover. Will had said to her: "I am not a man to hold
love lightly; it is no jest with me. My love is the one woman in the
whole world to me above all others. I shall love you living and dying,
and be true to you in life and death." The words rang in her ears like
sweetest music.

<span about="#mwt39"> </span>

There were extra services at all the colored churches that night,
because owing to the fact that most of the worshippers were employed as
<span id="c18_Contending_Forces_Chapter_18.xhtml#id-316"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="316"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/324"
page-index="324" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/324"></span>domestic
servants, evening was the only time convenient for their employers to
spare their services. She heard the outer door close upon one after
another until the house settled down to the perfect quiet of solitude.

<span about="#mwt39"> </span>

She then arose from her seat and proceeded to change her street attire
for a pale blue cashmere house dress with delicate ecru lace at the
throat and wrists. She hummed strains of the *Gloria* heard that morning
in church as she stood before the glass and took the pins from her hair.
The shining coil of burnished gold fell almost to her feet. So she stood
with the brushes poised in air as the door opened softly and John
Langley came quickly into her room. Thus they stood for a brief second
of time: she colorless with amazement and fear; he overwhelmed with the
exquisiteness of the picture before him. The brushes falling from her
hands broke the silence. Anger flamed in her eyes.

<span about="#mwt39"> </span>

"I did not hear you knock, Mr. Langley; what can I do for you?"

<span about="#mwt39"> </span>

His face was pale, his eyes wavering and bloodshot; his whole expression
indicated a terrible determination. The almost insulting coldness of her
tone exasperated him.

<span about="#mwt39"> </span>

"I did not knock," he replied, as he stepped into the room. "You do not
offer me a seat," he sneered, as he sat down on the couch.

<span about="#mwt39"> </span>

<span id="c18_Contending_Forces_Chapter_18.xhtml#id-317"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="317"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/325"
page-index="325" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/325"></span>"This
is hardly the time or place for parlor civilities," she replied
significantly. "Do you not know that the house is empty?"

<span about="#mwt46"> </span>

"I do; and as I wish to speak with you privately, and as this is my only
chance, I will not let it slip me," said John.

<span about="#mwt46"> </span>

"Really! I cannot understand what private business you can have with
me," returned Sappho haughtily; "but if it be so, pray state the nature
of it as quickly as possible."

<span about="#mwt46"> </span>

"I will," he replied. He paused a moment, as if collecting his forces.
"You and Will seem to have come to an understanding, judging by what I
saw on the Public Garden this morning," he said finally.

<span about="#mwt46"> </span>

Sappho flushed warmly as she replied: "And in what way does that concern
you?"

<span about="#mwt46"> </span>

The girl looked so beautiful and spirited that he longed to crush her
with the weight of his surmises.

<span about="#mwt46"> </span>

"Will Smith is a very proud man as well as a very just man. His pride of
family is his besetting sin. Do you think that it will be for his best
interest to marry you?" he asked significantly.

<span about="#mwt46"> </span>

"And why should it not be?" questioned the girl haughtily, although a
hand of ice seemed closing about her heart.

<span about="#mwt46"> </span>

"*You* can best answer that question," was the guarded reply.

<span about="#mwt46"> </span>

<span id="c18_Contending_Forces_Chapter_18.xhtml#id-318"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="318"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/326"
page-index="326" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/326"></span>"Mr.
Langley, your intrusion into my private apartment is unpardonable; your
innuendoes are still more so. If you have anything to say, please say
it; if not, let us close this remarkable interview." She moved toward
the door as if to open it. In an instant he seized her arm and stopped
her.

<span about="#mwt48"> </span>

"Do not be rash. It is better for you to listen to me and not arouse my
enmity. *I have seen Luke Sawyer, and I know your secret*—<span
class="smallcaps">Mabelle Beaubean!</span>

<span about="#mwt48"> </span>

The attack was so sudden she did not think of defending herself. He had
never in his life seen emotion so terrible depicted upon the human
countenance as her face presented at that moment. She shrank from him
with shivering dread, beating the air with both hands, as though in her
agony she warded off the approach of some gruesome apparition.

<span about="#mwt48"> </span>

"Not that name—not that name!" she gasped, as she turned her beautiful,
ghastly face from side to side, as though seeking air. She seemed about
to faint. Then she sank upon the floor and literally grovelled there,
her face hidden by the long hair which enveloped her like a veil.

<span about="#mwt48"> </span>

John was nonplussed at the perfect success of his plot; he was exultant.
The terror of the girl was awful to behold. Surely he had her now. She
must turn to him for help.

<span about="#mwt48"> </span>

<span id="c18_Contending_Forces_Chapter_18.xhtml#id-319"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="319"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/327"
page-index="327" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/327"></span>"Sappho,"
he said in feigned pity, "I feel for you. God knows I am sorry for this,
and that I should have been the one to bring it upon you. But think of
your story, and its effect upon Will if he should learn it after
marriage!"

<span about="#mwt50"> </span>

"I was a victim! an innocent child!" moaned the figure on the floor.

<span about="#mwt50"> </span>

"I know," said John, "but girls of fourteen are frequently wives in our
Southern climes, where women mature early. A man as supercilious as Will
in his pride of Northern birth would take no excuse, and would never
forgive."

<span about="#mwt50"> </span>

"Ah! what can I do?" cried the unhappy woman, as she rose to her feet
and began to pace the floor. "Have I not suffered enough?" Now was the
time for which he had waited and planned.

<span about="#mwt50"> </span>

"Sappho, do not think that I forget how abominable I must appear to you.
I know what you will say—that I betray my friend, and worse than that,
the girl who holds my promise; but my excuse is that my passion is
stronger than honor, stronger than reason. There are men in the world
who would not forsake you, and who would always befriend you. *Give up
Will, and trust yourself to me.* I am making money in my profession. I
can and will do well by you. Your story need never be known."

<span about="#mwt50"> </span>

<span id="c18_Contending_Forces_Chapter_18.xhtml#id-320"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="320"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/328"
page-index="328" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/328"></span>"Doubtless
you mean it in kindness," replied the girl wearily, "and I thank you,
but I can never marry anyone but Will."

<span about="#mwt52"> </span>

"Marriage!" exclaimed John, "who spoke of marriage? Ambitious men do not
marry women with stories like yours!"

<span about="#mwt52"> </span>

At last the murder was out—his meaning clear!

<span about="#mwt52"> </span>

For one moment Sappho was speechless with disgust.

<span about="#mwt52"> </span>

"Infamous villain!" she said at length, "you abuse my forlorn condition.
Leave me! Never till this moment have I realized the depths of my
degradation!"

<span about="#mwt52"> </span>

"I go at your command," said John. "I give you one week to think over
what I have said. At the end of that time"—

<span about="#mwt52"> </span>

"Go! go! will you go?" interrupted Sappho, pointing to the door with
queenly dignity.

<span about="#mwt52"> </span>

With a low bow John Langley passed out and closed the door behind him.

Sappho turned the key in the lock and sank upon the couch overcome with
the horror of her situation. Nervous spasms shook her frame as she
mentally measured the abyss of social ostracism and disgrace which
confronted her. A thunderbolt from a clear sky had shattered her hopes
and dashed the cup of happiness from her lips. Heartrending sobs of
anguish <span id="c18_Contending_Forces_Chapter_18.xhtml#Img-n320"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="Img"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/329"
page-index="329" page-quality="4"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/329"></span>

<figure>
<img src="images/c32_tending_Forces___Page_320.png_utm_source_en.wikisource.org_utm_campaign_parser_utm_content_thumbnail" class="mw-file-element" alt="" /><figcaption>"GO! GO! WILL YOU GO?" INTERRUPTED SAPPHO.</figcaption>
</figure>

<span about="#mwt54"> </span>

<span style="font-size:83%;">"GO! GO! WILL YOU GO?" INTERRUPTED
SAPPHO.</span>

<span id="c18_Contending_Forces_Chapter_18.xhtml#id-321"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="321"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/331"
page-index="331" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/331"></span>mingled
with passionate appeals to heaven for mercy, tore her frame as she
contemplated this sudden overthrow of her dreams of a bright future. The
quiet which had attended her life since she had settled in Boston was
rudely interrupted, and the ruin which threatened her was the more
stupendous because unlooked for in the direction from which it came.

<span about="#mwt58"> </span>

She got up at length, and going to the dressing-table, began
mechanically to collect her jewelry. She caught sight of her face in the
glass. What a change from the glowing beauty of one short hour ago! She
looked at the pale, drawn countenance with scorn. "Men call me
beautiful," she murmured bitterly; "what has beauty been to me but a
curse?"

<span about="#mwt58"> </span>

She heard the closing of doors and the voices of the returned
church-goers. Could it be possible that she was the same happy girl
whose smiling face had been given back from the mirror so radiantly a
short hour ago? She heard the front door close again. It was Will’s step
upon the stair. He paused at her door a moment, and then passed on to
the floor above. How often she had listened for that familiar tread. Her
heart kept repeating: "Never again—never again."

<span about="#mwt58"> </span>

Two hours later deep silence had fallen upon the house. A form attired
in black, closely <span
id="c18_Contending_Forces_Chapter_18.xhtml#id-322"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="322"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/332"
page-index="332" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/332"></span>veiled,
and carrying a traveling bag, passed from No. 500 D Street into the
night, boarded an electric car, and was swallowed up in the heart of the
metropolis.

<span about="#mwt58"> </span>

Will in his room had fallen into his first sleep when he thought he
heard the sound of noiseless footsteps in the hall. He listened a
moment, and then concluded that he was mistaken. Who could be moving
through the house at that hour? No, he was mistaken. His thoughts were
all with Sappho. He lived over again the words and scenes of the
morning. He heard the clocks strike twelve, one, two; then he fell into
a deep, dreamless sleep.

<span about="#mwt58"> </span>

<span id="c19_Contending_Forces_Chapter_19.xhtml"></span>

<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt1"></span>

<span id="c19_Contending_Forces_Chapter_19.xhtml#id-323"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="323"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/333"
page-index="333" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/333" about="#mwt4"
typeof="mw:Transclusion"></span>

<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt5"
typeof="mw:Transclusion"></span>

CHAPTER XIX.

<span style="font-size:92%;">THE BITTER ARROW.</span>

<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt5"></span>

<span class="mw-empty-elt"></span>

<span class="ws-poem-line">[<span class="smallcaps">O, Breaking</span>
heart that will not
break,](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Poems_(Tennyson,_1843)/Volume_1/The_Ballad_of_Oriana#ContendingForcesp323 "Poems (Tennyson, 1843)/Volume 1/The Ballad of Oriana")<span
class="ws-poem-break">  
</span></span><span class="ws-poem-line"><span class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:6em"></span>Oriana! <span
class="ws-poem-break">  
</span></span><span class="ws-poem-line">O, pale, pale face so sweet and
meek,<span class="ws-poem-break">  
</span></span><span class="ws-poem-line"><span class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:6em"></span>Oriana! <span
class="ws-poem-break">  
</span></span><span class="ws-poem-line ws-poem-right">—[<span
class="smallcaps">Tennyson</span>.](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Author:Alfred_Tennyson "Author:Alfred Tennyson")<span
class="ws-poem-break">  
</span></span><span class="ws-poem-break">  
</span>

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

<span class="smallcaps">The</span> next morning found the household
early astir. It is a rule in hotels that a "late watch," as it is
called, exempts the employee from an early appearance for duty the next
morning. On these mornings Will breakfasted with his mother and sister.
Since Sappho had boarded with them this humble meal had become a feast
to him.

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

He arose very early on this eventful Monday morning and hurried down the
stairs, bursting to impart his good news to the sympathetic ears of his
loved ones. He entered the kitchen just as Dora was laying the cloth for
breakfast.

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

"What in the world brings you down so early?" she asked in a surprised
tone as he entered the room.

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

"Oh, I couldn't stay in bed this morning of all others," he said, as he
kissed her lightly on the cheek; "I am too happy."

<span about="#mwt5"> </span>

<span id="c19_Contending_Forces_Chapter_19.xhtml#id-324"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="324"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/334"
page-index="334" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/334"></span>"What
under the sun is the cause of so much joy, that it brings so lazy a boy
out of bed an hour before his time?"

<span about="#mwt10"> </span>

"How did you feel when you were first engaged to John? weren’t you
light-headed and silly?"

<span about="#mwt10"> </span>

"Oh, I dare say I was silly enough," replied Dora with a sigh. At the
mention of John a cloud seemed to settle down upon the brightness of her
usually sunny face. "But what has that to do with your early rising? I
don’t see the analogy between the two."

<span about="#mwt10"> </span>

"Congratulate me, Dora, Sappho has promised to marry me."

<span about="#mwt10"> </span>

"Oh, Will! Why did I not think of that? I declare I suspected something
of the sort yesterday, but other things put it out of my mind. I am so
glad!" cried Dora. "Where—when—how did it occur?"

<span about="#mwt10"> </span>

"Yesterday on the Public Garden. I met her coming from church," he
replied proudly. "Do you see the analogy now between your engagement and
my early rising?" he added, laughing.

<span about="#mwt10"> </span>

"I should say so! How selfish of you to keep it to yourself all this
time; you might have told us. Mother! mother! come quickly, I have
something to tell you!" she called, moving toward the next room.

<span about="#mwt10"> </span>

<span id="c19_Contending_Forces_Chapter_19.xhtml#id-325"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="325"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/335"
page-index="335" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/335"></span>"Yes,
Dora, dear," replied the voice of Ma Smith, "just a minute!"

<span about="#mwt12"> </span>

"Oh, hurry, hurry, mummie; it won’t keep."

<span about="#mwt12"> </span>

"Yes, yes, you excitable child; what is it?" Nothing wrong with your
brother"—she stopped as she caught sight of his smiling face. He went up
to her and took her in his strong young arms—he was an affectionate son.

<span about="#mwt12"> </span>

"Nothing wrong, mummie dear, but your boy is very happy. Will you have
Sappho for a second daughter?"

<span about="#mwt12"> </span>

"Gladly, my son," replied his mother; and then she sank upon a chair,
overcome as she comprehended the full meaning of his words.

<span about="#mwt12"> </span>

"Why do you cry, mother dear, if the idea is not unpleasant to you?"
asked Will, somewhat alarmed by her tears.

<span about="#mwt12"> </span>

"Oh, Will, my boy, you will not let this new love cause you to forget
the love of your old mother?"

<span about="#mwt12"> </span>

"Never!" cried Will, as he pressed his lips to the sweet old face. "I
would not be worthy of happiness could I be so ungrateful."

<span about="#mwt12"> </span>

"Don’t mind mother, Will; she cries on principle, because it is the
proper thing for weddings and funerals. Isn’t that so, mummie mine?"
laughed Dora, as she wiped a suspicious moisture from her own eyes.

<span about="#mwt12"> </span>

"But what keeps Sappho so late? she’s gen<span
id="c19_Contending_Forces_Chapter_19.xhtml#id-326"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="326"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/336"
page-index="336" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/336"></span>erally
down by this time," she continued, with a glance at the little Swiss
clock on the mantel.

<span about="#mwt12"> </span>

"I'm going up stairs after the laggard, and have it out with her for
keeping such news to herself. I know she’s bashful."

<span about="#mwt12"> </span>

Dora sprang up the stairs with a bound, and paused outside the door to
rap. Receiving no answer she rapped again, and then tried the handle of
the door. It yielded to her touch, and then she noticed that the key was
on the outside. She threw the door open with a vague sense of something
unusual in such a proceeding. The room was empty. She paused a moment in
bewilderment as she glanced about her, and then saw the bed undisturbed
in its snowy smoothness. Thoroughly alarmed now, she ran to the head of
the stairs and called her brother.

<span about="#mwt12"> </span>

Wondering what could be the trouble, Will hurried from the kitchen,
closely followed by his mother. Upstairs they found Dora standing in the
middle of the room with a frightened face.

<span about="#mwt12"> </span>

"I found the key on the outside, the door unlocked. She is not here; and
see, the bed has not been slept in; the closet door is open; some of her
clothing is gone, and all her jewelry."

<span about="#mwt12"> </span>

Will stood beside her, gazing about the cozy nest with troubled eyes.

<span about="#mwt12"> </span>

<span id="c19_Contending_Forces_Chapter_19.xhtml#id-327"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="327"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/337"
page-index="337" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/337"></span>"When
did you see her last?" he asked finally.

<span about="#mwt16"> </span>

"At dinner yesterday; she complained of a headache, and went to her room
to lie down."

<span about="#mwt16"> </span>

"She was here. at half-past eleven, when I came in, for I saw a light in
her window as I came down the street."

<span about="#mwt16"> </span>

"Well, children, there is nothing to worry about," said Mrs. Smith; "she
has probably gone out and will return shortly. There can’t be any great
mystery about her absence."

<span about="#mwt16"> </span>

Just then the door-bell rang.

<span about="#mwt16"> </span>

"There she is now,” said Will, as he bounded down the stairs in answer
to the summons. The two women stood expectant, waiting for the sound of
her voice. They heard nothing but the closing of the door and Will’s
steps returning. Presently he stood before them with a letter in his
hand.

<span about="#mwt16"> </span>

"It is for me," he said, in answer to their looks of inquiry. "I think
it is from Sappho."

<span about="#mwt16"> </span>

His face was ashen; his hands trembled. He turned the square package
over once—again—then mechanically, as in a dream, he tore the envelope
open. The anxious women watched him as he ran his eyes rapidly over the
written page, and then with a groan sank into a chair.

<span about="#mwt16"> </span>

"What is it, Will? what has happened?" <span
id="c19_Contending_Forces_Chapter_19.xhtml#id-328"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="328"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/338"
page-index="338" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/338"></span>cried
his mother and sister with one voice. Great drops of perspiration stood
upon his forehead.

<span about="#mwt16"> </span>

"O my God!" was his reply to the agonized entreaties of the two women.
He threw his arms upon the desk beside him and dropped his head upon
them. The letter fell upon the floor.

<span about="#mwt16"> </span>

Mrs. Smith was a great sufferer from nervous weakness. Seeing her son’s
unhappiness, she began to tremble and sob hysterically. Dora was badly
frightened; but knowing that she must be calm for all their sakes, said:

<span about="#mwt16"> </span>

"Mother, calm yourself. Do you not see that Will needs help?" She closed
the open door to keep out curious intruders who might possibly be about,
and then she picked up the letter.

<span about="#mwt16"> </span>

"Read it!" groaned Will; "I can never tell you."

<span about="#mwt16"> </span>

Dora read it aloud:

<span about="#mwt16"> </span>

<span class="smallcaps">Will:</span>

When you receive this I shall be far away. What I am writing to you I
ought to have told your family long ago. It must forever stand between
us and marriage.

You remember the story told the night of the public meeting of the
League by Luke Sawyer—the night I fainted? *I am the unfortunate Mabelle
Beaubean!*

I did not die. The good Sisters gave out that story <span
id="c19_Contending_Forces_Chapter_19.xhtml#id-329"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="329"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/339"
page-index="339" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/339"></span>in
order to destroy my identity. Madam Frances is my grand-aunt. The boy
Alphonse is my child! O my God! how can I live and write these words?

John Langley came to my room tonight, after everyone was at church. He
has found out my story, and has offered me the greatest insult that a
man can give a woman. He has made me realize how much such a marriage
with me would injure you. Disgrace shall never touch you or yours
through me.

Forgive me for deceiving you—for loving you; it is all the wrong that I
have done you.

O love—my love—how can I give you up? You will never know the bitterness
of the step I am taking!

<span class="smallcaps">Sappho.</span>

<span about="#mwt16"> </span>

Mrs. Smith shuddered as she listened to the words being read. Could it
be possible that this strange, unexpected confession had come from the
beautiful girl they knew so well?

<span about="#mwt16"> </span>

Dora was weeping bitterly when she finished; so was her mother. They sat
there stunned into silence. Every pulse beat at fever-heat in Will’s
body. His nerves were strained almost beyond endurance; they were a
thousand needle-points of pain. How could he bear this terrible news
that had come to him! Luke Sawyer’s story was written before his eyes in
letters of fire. He saw with acute clairvoyant sight the beautiful
child—the brutal captor—the horrid surroundings of the vile den to which
he had consigned her—her struggles within <span
id="c19_Contending_Forces_Chapter_19.xhtml#id-330"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="330"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/340"
page-index="340" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/340"></span>the
brutal grasp—all the sickening details of that horrible violation of
chastity were mirrored within his brain. Time could never eradicate
them. God of heaven! how could he bear to think that his beautiful
Sappho—his pure girl-love, had been subjected to such brutality! And
then the child! Agony of agonies! He could not suffer more and live!

<span about="#mwt16"> </span>

There was something awe-inspiring in his great affliction to the women
who loved him. Dismal silence had seized upon them all. Then a
sob—dreadful to hear—broke from Will. He staggered across the room and
fell on his knees by his mother’s side and buried his face in the folds
of her dress. It was a way he had in early childhood when anything
distressed him. Then his mother comforted him as in childhood days—his
head upon her tender breast—her dear wrinkled hands, as if in blessing,
resting upon his grief-stricken brow.

<span about="#mwt16"> </span>

At length Dora broke the silence.

<span about="#mwt16"> </span>

"Oh, that poor, miserable girl! think of her sufferings—of the weight of
the secret she carried with her. What a crucifixion for a proud spirit
like hers! This terrible curse of slavery! shall we never lose the sting
of degradation? Think of John, too! How did he find it out? what a
heartless scamp he must be! I confess the whole thing is an enigma to
me."

<span about="#mwt16"> </span>

<span id="c19_Contending_Forces_Chapter_19.xhtml#id-331"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="331"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/341"
page-index="341" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/341"></span>“To
think she should believe us capable of feeling anything for her but
sympathy! You must find her, Will, and bring her back home,” said Ma
Smith.

<span about="#mwt26"> </span>

“Yes, mother; but any girl would act as she has. Think of her
humiliation to be obliged to own such a story to her lover! I do not
wonder that she turned a coward and fled. But Will will find her, never
fear.”

<span about="#mwt26"> </span>

Will had regained something of self-control. But what a change had come
to that debonair countenance! Dora’s heart ached as she looked at him.
He arose from his knees by his mother’s side and stood a moment as if
bewildered.

<span about="#mwt26"> </span>

“I am going to hire the best detective in the city to find her. But
first I will settle with John Langley,” he said at last.

<span about="#mwt26"> </span>

“Give him this!” cried Dora, snatching her engagement ring from her
finger. “Tell him I hope never to see his face again.”

<span about="#mwt26"> </span>

“Poor little sister. Must you suffer, too?”

<span about="#mwt26"> </span>

“Don’t pity me, Will!” she exclaimed with flashing eyes. “I am well rid
of such a man.”

<span about="#mwt26"> </span>

Will kissed them both solemnly and went out.

<span about="#mwt26"> </span>

The two women, left alone, went softly about the pretty room folding
away the scattered articles of wearing apparel, which they watered <span
id="c19_Contending_Forces_Chapter_19.xhtml#id-332"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="332"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/342"
page-index="342" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/342"></span>with
many bitter tears, and finally closing the blinds and drawing down the
curtains. She will return, they told each other, hoping against hope. We
will leave things as they are. And then went out and locked the door.

<span about="#mwt26"> </span>

<span class="wst-asterisks tiInherit">•<span class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•</span>

<span about="#mwt26"> </span>

Such incidents as we have pictured above are not uncommon in any
community where slavery has cast its baleful shadow. Emancipation has
done much, but time and moral training among the white men of the South
are the only cures for concubinage.

<span about="#mwt26"> </span>

We may right a wrong, but we cannot restore our victim to his primeval
state of happiness. Something is lost that can never be regained. The
wages of sin is death. Innocent or guilty, the laws of nature are
immutable. So with shoulders bent and misshapen with heavy burdens, the
Negro plods along bearing his cross—carrying *the sins of others*.

<span about="#mwt26"> </span>

Time was no more. I stood there gazing at the approaching forms of
matchless beauty advancing toward the judgment seat of God. And I asked
a question: “Who are these arrayed in white?” And one of the elders
answered: “These are they who come out of great tribulation!”

<span about="#mwt26"> </span>

<span class="wst-asterisks tiInherit">•<span class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•</span>

<span about="#mwt26"> </span>

<span id="c19_Contending_Forces_Chapter_19.xhtml#id-333"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="333"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/343"
page-index="343" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/343"></span>Fortunately
it happened that it was an hour when the inmates of No. 500 D Street
were busy attending to their various vocations. No one need know more of
Sappho’s disappearance than the family felt to tell. They made one
exception to this resolve. It was about ten o'clock when Doctor Lewis
called. The mother and daughter were just sitting down to the meal so
sadly interrupted. In answer to questions asked in friendly solicitude
because of their troubled looks, the whole miserable story came out.

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

“It is fortunate that I happened in,” he said, when he had heard all
they could tell him.

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

“I will go right down to Langley’s office. It is useless to allow more
trouble to grow out of this unhappy affair. Those men must not meet
alone.”

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

Amazed, startled, dumbfounded at this romance in real life, he hurried
away toward the business portion of the city. But under his anxiety to
serve his friends, who could blame him if thoughts of the broken
engagement and all that it meant to him would intrude upon him? His
heart kept singing: “Free to be won—free to be won.”

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

<span class="wst-asterisks tiInherit">•<span class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•</span>

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

John had risen early and left the house before any one was astir. He
could not sleep. <span
id="c19_Contending_Forces_Chapter_19.xhtml#id-334"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="334"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/344"
page-index="344" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/344"></span>Up and
down the deserted streets he paced, gazing at the silent stars and
baring his head to the cold, refreshing breeze of the April morning.
Haunted—tormented—conscience not yet sufficiently seared to commit so
mean an act without regret, would be heard. What if the girl should defy
him—expose him! He cursed his own folly, her beauty, his own
infatuation. Turn as he would, he could not shut out the beautiful,
despairing face.

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

<span class="ws-poem-line">“And there rises ever a passionate cry <span
class="ws-poem-break">  
</span></span><span class="ws-poem-line">From underneath in the darkened
land”—<span class="ws-poem-break">  
</span></span><span class="ws-poem-break">  
</span>

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

There is something magical in the mystery of night and the shadows of
early dawn. Then the mystery of creation and our own hidden nature seem
to be revealed faintly to our dull sensibilities. The soul beholds
itself all unadorned as it must stand before Almighty God. He raised his
eyes to heaven and there beheld the beauty of myriad stars and
undiscovered worlds bespangling the firmament. Never before had he
noticed their sublimity. By the tremulous half-light of the early dawn
his deed of the night before appeared despicable—devilish. For a time
his head dropped in shame before the accusations of his soul. There came
dimly to his beclouded faculties a realization of the possibilities of a
life which might have been his under opposite conditions <span
id="c19_Contending_Forces_Chapter_19.xhtml#id-335"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="335"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/345"
page-index="345" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/345"></span>from
those in which he was born. Events of his childhood in the South came to
him through the mist of years. He saw himself a half-starved beggar in
the city streets, a deserted child claiming kindred with none, allowed a
shelter in a poor Negro cabin for charity’s sake, begging his bread from
the generous passer-by. Somehow he had learned his letters. Then he
became ambitious to learn more. He heard talk of a country to the north
which seemed to his childish imagination a fairyland. He determined to
go there; so he started on his wearisome journey at an age when the
loving mother trembles to have her darling exposed to the perils of the
busy streets without a strong hand to guide him through its dangers. All
his capital was in his nimble baby feet. He charmed coin from the people
wherever he went by his expert dancing. He could do the breakdown and
many intricate shuffles in a way that delighted his audiences. When he
reached the North he managed to buy a bootblack’s outfit; then he worked
his way into a hotel kitchen. He went on from one thing to another,
always keeping the one end in view—to acquire an education.

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

He had prospered. He had accomplished the acquisition of knowledge at
the expense of the non-development of every moral faculty. <span
id="c19_Contending_Forces_Chapter_19.xhtml#id-336"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="336"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/346"
page-index="346" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/346"></span>He did
not realize that he was a responsible being, or that morality was
obligatory upon him. With him, might was right. This man was what he was
through the faults of others. If there be truth in the assertion that we
create ourselves in the life we live on earth, the sphere that makes our
heaven or hell within the illimitable realms of futurity, then it may be
that the pitying eye of Heaven sees the struggles of these irresponsible
ones of earth who are monuments of man’s inhumanity to man, and sends a
warning, pleading voice along the dim, resounding aisles of
self-communion. It is a law of the All-wise that Nemesis shall walk
beside us in every wrong that we commit. For joy we pay, for sorrow,
too, and also for the ill we do. We call the recoil which comes from
evil-doing, retribution, which being the fruit of evil, is co-existent
with it. John had given no thought to the needs of his soul in his
pursuit of wealth and position. Tonight he had the feeling that he had
given all for nothing; that in his blind, egotistical seeking for his
own gratification he had overstepped the bounds, and that retribution
would put a check upon his desires.

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

That night his better angel triumphed, and he resolved that he would
apologize to Sappho, make up with Dora, and tell Will all his surmises
about Mr, Withington. But when day<span
id="c19_Contending_Forces_Chapter_19.xhtml#id-337"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="337"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/347"
page-index="347" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/347"></span>light
flooded the city he had resolved to let matters remain as they were.
Love, honors and a competency were his for the taking, but he had risked
all—for what? He would soon know.

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

<span class="wst-asterisks tiInherit">•<span class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•<span
class="wst-gap __gap"
style="display:inline-block; inline-size:2em"></span>•</span>

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

Ten o'clock found him sitting listlessly before his desk. It happened
that he had no cases in court that morning. He pushed his work one side;
he could not think; ever before his eyes was a vision in pale blue, with
floating golden hair and appealing eyes. His hand shook as if with the
palsy as he raised his pen to dip it in the ink-well. With an oath he
dashed it from him. He did not indulge in liquor as a rule, but he made
up his mind to have a glass of whiskey when he went to lunch. Presently
he heard the door open. He looked up at the sound, and met the eyes of
the friend he had betrayed—Will Smith stood before him! John sprang to
his feet. His eyes dropped before the steady regard of the other. Thus
they stood a moment. At last Will broke the painful silence.

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

“What have I ever done to you, John, that you should treat me as you
have?”

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

“What do you mean?” stammered John.

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

“Do you ask me that?”

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

“I do,” came faintly from John’s lips.

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

“I will reply by asking you another question. <span
id="c19_Contending_Forces_Chapter_19.xhtml#id-338"
class="pagenum ws-pagenum" page-number="338"
page-name="Page:Contending Forces by Pauline Hopkins.djvu/348"
page-index="348" page-quality="3"
title="Page:Contending_Forces_by_Pauline_Hopkins.djvu/348"></span>Has
your love for Dora turned to hate, that you can deliberately humiliate
the girl you have wished to make your wife, as you have her?”

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

John’s courage seemed to have deserted him. He stood before Smith like
the culprit that he was.

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

“Of what do you accuse me?” he cried at length, in desperation.

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

“Does not your conscience answer? If not, read that,” and Will handed
him Sappho’s letter.

<span about="#mwt32"> </span>

John took it, and even as his hand grasped the sheet of paper he began
